a16z Podcast 2 episode(s) · 48 insights

Why AI’s Next Breakthroughs Could Come from Outside the Big Labs55 min23 insights2026-09-26
▶ Watch on YouTube
Three a16z voices (one with a nuclear-weapons-lab security background, one a former Microsoft executive who lived through the antitrust era, and a third co-host called 'Aaron' in the transcript) dissect Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei's 'pacing' proposal, arguing its substance is reasonable but its messaging invites the exact heavy-handed, EU-style regulation it hopes to avoid. The conversation pivots to how AI agent swarms break enterprise security assumptions and closes on a technical thesis: a new probabilistic-classifier technique for embedding language models into traditional software (credited to 'Jeff') is spreading faster than any AI adoption since ChatGPT and is happening outside the frontier labs, which the speakers frame as evidence the center of AI innovation has shifted to the application/platform layer.
The 'Pacing' Post: Reasonable Substance, Broken Atmospherics · 4Nationalization Precedents for Critical AI Infrastructure · 2Regulate Too Early, Regulate Too Late: The Historical Trade-off · 3Vocabulary Capture and the Coming 'GDPR for AI' · 5AI Agent Swarms Break the Enterprise Security Model · 4What Computer-Crime Law History Says About AI Liability · 2Beyond Chat: A Probabilistic-Classifier Trick Moves Innovation Outside the Labs · 3
The 'Pacing' Post: Reasonable Substance, Broken Atmospherics 4
Martin Casado argues that 'pacing' is a logically incoherent framing for AI safety because pacing is orthogonal to security — slowing the build rate of a dangerous system doesn't make it safer, just as building a nuclear weapon slowly rather than quickly doesn't reduce its danger.
contrarian🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: highDario AmodeiAI safety regulation
So what: Investors watching AI-safety-driven regulatory risk should note that 'pacing' commitments are optics, not a real slowdown in capability development — don't price them as a brake on frontier-lab progress.
“you can very slowly build a nuclear weapon and that doesn't make anybody feel better that it's slow versus fast” ▶ 4:40
Martin Casado contends the 'pacing' claim is factually dubious: the labs referenced have raised more capital and grown faster than ever before, with no observable slowdown — the only real change is a slower cadence of external model releases, not internal capability development.
data_point🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: highAI labsfundraising
So what: Capital markets should treat 'pacing' announcements as PR rather than a signal that frontier AI capex or capability velocity is actually decelerating.
“They've been at a dead run. They've raised more money than ever before. They've grown faster than ever before. There's no indication that they're pacing.” ▶ 8:45
The co-hosts argue that Dario Amodei's 'pacing' safety post, published the same week he separately stated on television that he believes there is roughly a 10% chance of species extinction from AI, looks like an inadequate half-measure ('milktoast capitulation') given the scale of the risk he claims to believe in — a messaging mismatch, not a substance problem.
contrarian🗣 Aaron (co-host)conviction: highDario Amodei
So what: A credibility gap between a lab CEO's public risk estimate and the lab's actual proposed remedy is a reputational and regulatory-invitation risk investors in AI labs should track.
“this post that he has looks like this milktoast capitulation that's totally not adequate for the task at hand” ▶ 4:16
Martin Casado frames labs' refusal to state an official position on existential (x-)risk as deliberate: once a lab admits the risk of catastrophe is non-zero, the only logically coherent policy response is nationalization, so ambiguity lets labs avoid that burden while still invoking the risk narrative when convenient.
framework🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: highx-risknationalization
So what: Watch for any frontier lab explicitly quantifying extinction risk publicly — per this logic it would be a strong forcing function toward nationalization-style regulation of that lab's business.
“the only answer is you have to nationalize it” ▶ 16:17
Nationalization Precedents for Critical AI Infrastructure 2
One co-host observes that since the post-World War II era, most US industries deemed critical infrastructure — power, banking, and healthcare — have trended toward de facto nationalization through heavy regulation even without formal government ownership, suggesting frontier AI labs may be angling to be treated like regulated-but-private infrastructure (their own comparison: JPMorgan or Verizon) rather than risk outright nationalization.
framework🗣 Aaron (co-host)conviction: mediumbankinghealthcarepower
So what: If AI labs pursue 'critical infrastructure' positioning, expect them to lobby for bank-like or telecom-like regulatory regimes rather than antitrust breakup or state ownership — a materially better outcome for equity holders.
“since the post World War II era most industries that are critical to the infrastructure power and banking and healthcare. The trend has been to basically be nationalized.” ▶ 17:16
Martin Casado argues FINRA — an industry-funded but government-mandated self-regulatory body for securities firms — is likely the 'best case scenario' template for future AI regulation once AI is embedded in healthcare recommendations, implanted medical devices, high-frequency trading, and aviation systems, because full nationalization would otherwise become politically unavoidable.
prediction🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: mediumFINRAhealthcarehigh-frequency trading
So what: A FINRA-style self-regulatory organization for AI would be a materially more favorable regulatory outcome than nationalization or EU-style prescriptive rules — a scenario to watch for as an investable 'best case' regulatory path.
“FINRA actually is like the probably the best case scenario of what that looks like” ▶ 22:11
Regulate Too Early, Regulate Too Late: The Historical Trade-off 3
Citing the history of aviation regulation — no pilot's license required until the 1920s and no real airworthiness oversight until decades after the Wright brothers — the co-hosts argue the FAA became the safest-in-class regulator precisely because it arrived late, but at the direct cost of aviation now being the slowest-moving, most difficult sector for innovation, framing this as the core trade-off facing AI regulation timing.
framework🗣 Steven Sinofskyconviction: highFAAaviation
So what: Early, prescriptive AI regulation should be expected to trade near-term safety optics for long-run innovation velocity — a directly relevant precedent for evaluating proposed AI safety legislation's economic cost.
“it's also the slowest most difficult form of innovation” ▶ 47:30
Referencing a Nick Bostrom podcast, one co-host argues that regulating AI prematurely doesn't reduce the underlying risk because regulators would be imposing controls on a system whose behavior isn't yet understood — you end up with the technology plus a false sense of control rather than actual risk reduction.
thesis🗣 Aaron (co-host)conviction: mediumNick BostromAI regulation
So what: This argument, if it gains traction among policymakers, cuts against fast-tracked AI legislation and favors a wait-and-observe regulatory posture — relevant to handicapping near-term AI legislative risk.
“If you regulate AI too early, you actually don't solve anything. You still just kind of had the same risk ultimately.” ▶ 0:00
Steven Sinofsky recounts that before 2001, any Windows PC connected to the internet would get infected before installation even finished, and that the early internet era caused tens of billions of dollars in economic damage including infrastructure and hospital outages — yet regulating in that mid-1990s climate would likely have prevented the internet from developing into what it became.
anecdote🗣 Steven Sinofskyconviction: highinternetWindows
So what: A useful historical anchor for investors modeling AI regulatory risk: catastrophic-sounding early-stage technology harm rates have previously coexisted with, rather than prevented, massive subsequent value creation once security matured organically.
“we've caused tens of billions of dollars of economic damage” ▶ 23:44
Vocabulary Capture and the Coming 'GDPR for AI' 5
One co-host predicts the 2028 US election will function as a referendum on AI policy, but expects no candidate to run on an explicitly pro-AI platform because the pro-AI argument requires too much nuance to fit into a campaign message, while anti-AI framing is simple and already dominant.
prediction🗣 Aaron (co-host)conviction: medium2028 electionAI regulation
So what: Investors should expect AI policy risk to skew toward restriction rather than promotion heading into 2028, regardless of which party wins, since no major candidate is incentivized to campaign as pro-AI.
“the next election will 100% be a referendum on on AI” ▶ 12:07
The co-hosts argue the AI industry has already lost control of the debate's vocabulary — terms like 'pause,' 'swarms,' and 'rogue' were coined and popularized by AI critics — meaning winning the public policy argument now requires inventing new, more favorable framing rather than just presenting better facts.
framework🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: mediumAI regulationpublic messaging
So what: Track which side controls framing language in AI policy debates as a leading indicator of legislative direction — favorable vocabulary shifts (or their absence) from the industry are a soft signal for regulatory risk.
“Every word has been chosen by the people who don't want to do AI.” ▶ 12:56
One co-host argues that unresolved political frustration from earlier tech regulation misses — e.g., Congress admitting it failed to foresee the internet's scale when passing the Communications Decency Act, and Senator Elizabeth Warren's criticism of social media — has built up a reservoir of anti-tech political energy in the US Senate that is now being redirected into AI regulation rather than representing a fresh, AI-specific reaction.
causal🗣 Steven Sinofskyconviction: mediumElizabeth WarrenUS SenateCommunications Decency Act
So what: AI regulatory intensity in Congress may be driven more by lawmakers' unresolved grievances over social media than by AI-specific evidence — a factor that could make AI rules harsher or more symbolic than the actual risk profile warrants.
“there's a lot of pent-up energy against tech that that could end up just all siphoning into into AI right now” ▶ 23:23
Because the US ceased leading in tech antitrust roughly 15 years ago, the co-hosts predict the EU will take the lead in AI regulation with a 'GDPR for AI' approach, since Europe has little domestic AI industry to protect and therefore 'nothing to lose' by regulating aggressively.
prediction🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: highGDPREuropean Uniontech antitrust
So what: Global AI compliance costs and product design constraints are more likely to be set by EU regulators than US regulators — companies should plan for EU rules as the binding global constraint, similar to how GDPR shaped global data practices.
“The US about 15 years ago stopped leading in tech antitrust. The problem is that Europe is going to lead with that because they have nothing to lose.” ▶ 0:35
The co-hosts predict EU-style AI regulation will converge on consent-style prompts every time an AI agent 'emits text' to a third party or takes an external action, comparing this to cookie-consent banners and automobile airbag warning labels — low-value user friction that regulators favor because it creates an auditable liability trail even though users habitually click through it without reading.
prediction🗣 Steven Sinofskyconviction: mediumGDPREuropean Union
So what: Product teams building AI agents for the EU market should expect mandated per-action consent/warning UI patterns as a near-term compliance cost, with limited actual safety benefit — a design and margin consideration for AI product companies.
“becomes like a a a safety warning like the airbag thing in your car” ▶ 42:15
AI Agent Swarms Break the Enterprise Security Model 4
Martin Casado argues that enterprise security has historically relied on the assumption that malicious insiders are rare (roughly one in ten thousand) and that employees do the right thing 95-99% of the time; autonomous AI agent swarms invert this assumption because they act like roaming automated agents operating at roughly 10,000x the scale of a human workforce and will systematically misjudge which tasks are legitimate.
framework🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: highAI agentsenterprise security
So what: Cybersecurity vendors and enterprise IT budgets should anticipate a step-change in demand for agent-specific identity, authentication, and anomaly-detection tooling as legacy 'trust most insiders' security models stop working.
“Agent swarms completely flip that. These are just roaming drones but like times 10,000 and they will easily mistake a good task for a bad one.” ▶ 0:21
Martin Casado states that companies now need an entirely new internal security layer that tracks authentication events and API usage far more granularly than before, because routine internal tools — internal GitHub, Slack, expense systems — that were never designed to withstand high-volume automated access can now be inadvertently overwhelmed by agent swarms in a pattern indistinguishable from a denial-of-service attack.
causal🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: highAPI securityauthentication
So what: This is a direct product opportunity: enterprise API-authentication monitoring and agent-traffic-shaping tools are likely to see a fast-growing new demand category as AI agent deployment scales inside companies.
“we need a whole layer internally that just is tracking way more about what authentications are being done, what APIs are being done” ▶ 35:10
Both co-hosts argue current operating-system access-control models are too coarse for agentic AI — today's choices are essentially binary (ask the user for permission on every single action, or grant an agent unrestricted access that could delete an entire file system) — and that decades-old academic multi-level security (MLS) research, previously stalled by usability problems for human users, may finally become viable now that AI itself can manage the added complexity.
framework🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: mediumoperating systemsmulti-level security
So what: A revival of fine-grained, AI-managed access-control systems represents a potential new infrastructure layer/market for security startups, distinct from today's binary permission models.
“our OS probably wasn't built for the right level of of granular set of of tools you want to give the agent” ▶ 40:39
The co-hosts use a history of real covert-channel exfiltration techniques (sampling CRT raster-beam pixels through a window to reconstruct a screen, subverting individual pixels to encode messages, Tempest electromagnetic monitoring-distance attacks, BIOS spread-spectrum and audio-speaker side channels) to argue that speculative AI x-risk scenarios (e.g., a model exfiltrating itself via CPU heat) should be grounded in concrete, falsifiable engineering constraints like bit-rate and entropy rather than treated as unbounded hypotheticals.
anecdote🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: mediumcovert channelscybersecurity
So what: Investors evaluating AI-safety claims should favor teams and researchers who frame x-risk in measurable engineering terms (bandwidth, entropy, physical channel limits) over those using purely rhetorical or unfalsifiable risk framing.
“if you can subvert three pixels, you can use those, you know, because it just looks like bad pixels. You can use those to basically send a message.” ▶ 33:06
What Computer-Crime Law History Says About AI Liability 2
Martin Casado argues that roughly 90% of the legal framework needed to prosecute AI-related computer crime already exists at the 'application layer' (e.g., unauthorized computer access is already a federal crime under existing law), implying new AI-specific model-layer liability statutes are less urgently needed than commonly assumed.
framework🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: mediumcomputer crime lawAI liability
So what: Legislative risk from wholly new AI liability statutes may be overstated in the near term, since existing computer-crime law already covers most foreseeable bad-actor scenarios — relevant to handicapping the scope of pending AI legislation.
“we probably you know we probably have like 90% of laws already in the applied layer like you can't hack systems etc” ▶ 26:27
Martin Casado criticizes current AI lab security-incident postmortems as structurally deficient compared to the rigorous incident-reporting standards CERT/CMU established in the 1980s, describing lab disclosures as resembling externally-lawyered investigations that withhold internal detail rather than complete technical postmortems.
contrarian🗣 Martin Casadoconviction: highAI labsincident response
So what: Investors and enterprise buyers evaluating AI labs' security posture should discount the credibility of self-published incident postmortems until labs adopt CERT-style structured, complete disclosure practices.
“that looks like an intern wrote it and it's not a postmortem it's this selective memory” ▶ 28:16
Beyond Chat: A Probabilistic-Classifier Trick Moves Innovation Outside the Labs 3
A technique credited to 'Jeff' reframes how language models get embedded in traditional software: instead of generating expensive free-form text that a program then has to re-parse, the model reads input text and returns a probability against a fixed set of predefined options (e.g., '80% customer service'), which is described as faster, cheaper, more accurate, and the fastest-adopted AI integration pattern since ChatGPT.
data_point🗣 co-hostconviction: highlanguage modelsprobabilistic programming
So what: This classifier-style integration pattern is a concrete, near-term product/infrastructure opportunity for embedding LLMs into deterministic software pipelines at lower cost than full text generation — worth tracking as a distinct, fast-growing AI application category separate from chat products.
“this has probably been the fastest adoption of an AI model since chat GBT” ▶ 49:44
One co-host argues frontier AI labs have been slow to build this classifier-style, software-native integration because their internal culture is oriented toward 'creating beings' that communicate in natural language, rather than components designed to slot into traditional deterministic software — a structural blind spot relative to application-layer builders.
contrarian🗣 co-hostconviction: mediumAI labssoftware architecture
So what: This is a thesis for why the most useful near-term AI product innovation may keep coming from application-layer startups and platform companies rather than the foundation-model labs themselves — relevant to sourcing AI investment opportunities outside the big labs.
“if you're trying to create god god speaks in natural languages or whatever” ▶ 53:18
The co-hosts conclude that once an AI lab becomes a platform, it becomes structurally overwhelmed just maintaining scale, uptime, and compatibility — mirroring how Apple's ecosystem stops innovating at the platform layer and instead absorbs ('Sherlocks') successful third-party features — which they present as the signal that AI's center of innovation has moved to the application layer outside the frontier labs.
thesis🗣 co-hostconviction: highAppleAI platforms
So what: This is the episode's central investment thesis: allocate research and deal-sourcing attention toward application-layer AI companies solving integration problems (like the probabilistic-classifier trick), not just toward the foundation-model labs, as the more active innovation frontier.
“the center of innovation has just moved” ▶ 0:46
How to Spot Exceptional Talent Before Everyone Else50 min25 insights2026-09-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
a16z partners (including twins Olivia and Justine Moore, plus 'David' and 'Eric') unveil Cosign, a new AI-populated professional-reputation directory built on granular public/private endorsements ('who shaped your career', 'who you'd work with in the trenches', 'person to watch'), arguing that peer cosigns are becoming a more valuable and scarce signal than credentials or LinkedIn connections, especially as AI-driven outreach floods inboxes. Note: despite the title, the episode is a product-launch conversation about Cosign rather than a general talent-scouting how-to.

Cosign: a16z's reputation-network product 6
a16z is launching Cosign, a directory-style platform for professional reputation built on peer endorsements rather than institutional credentials, framed around the idea that being publicly followed/endorsed by figures like Patrick Collison, Elon Musk, or Marc Andreessen now signals more than a Stanford degree.
thesis🗣 Eric (a16z, introducing Cosign)conviction: highCosignPatrick CollisonElon MuskMarc AndreessenStanford
So what: Signals a broader shift toward peer-endorsement graphs as a credentialing layer; relevant for anyone building identity, recruiting, or reputation infrastructure.
“What's more impressive to you? Someone who went to Stanford or someone who is followed by Patrick Collison, Elon Musk, Mark Andre.” ▶ 0:10
Cosign's profile schema requires at least three endorsement types per person: who shaped your career (mentor), who you'd work with 'in the trenches,' and who is a 'person to watch' for future success — designed to be built up and rank-ordered over years, similar to how an investor track record accrues credibility.
framework🗣 Eric (a16z, Cosign product)conviction: highCosign
So what: A reusable schema for any reputation or credentialing product: separate mentor-signal, trust-signal, and forward-looking-conviction-signal rather than a single undifferentiated 'connection'.
Cosign is designed to solve the cold-start problem of reputation networks by using AI to scrape and structure already-public information (funding announcements, X/Twitter posts and quote-tweets) into pre-populated profiles, rather than requiring users to opt in first, including a live feed sourced from a direct data connection into X.
framework🗣 David/Eric (a16z, Cosign product)conviction: highCosignX
So what: AI-driven aggregation of public data is becoming a substitute for organic network effects, lowering the bar for launching reputation/social-graph products that would otherwise face a cold start.
“only you know because of AI can we generate all of this information on people and like recreate you know a professional graph” ▶ 43:47
Cosign is deliberately built to be all-positive, an explicit product trade-off ('no product can be all things to all people'), betting that people will use it to showcase real achievements rather than adding another venue for critique, which the team believes already has plenty of outlets online.
contrarian🗣 David (a16z, Cosign product)conviction: highCosign
So what: A specific, falsifiable product bet worth tracking: whether a purely positive-sentiment social feed can sustain daily engagement versus mixed-sentiment incumbents like X and LinkedIn.
“this product is going to win on people um using it to show off like to show off in a good way” ▶ 22:11
Cosign's core differentiation thesis is that valuable reputation signal (compliments, endorsements, career-defining moments) currently happens ephemerally on X and disappears within a day, and that permanently attaching those moments to a durable professional profile is itself the product's main value-add versus existing social feeds.
thesis🗣 Eric (a16z, Cosign product)conviction: highCosignX
So what: Identifies a specific unmet need — persistence of social proof — that competing 'reputation layer' or 'social CV' startups could also target.
“any uh announcement is going to be tied to their sort of, you know, durable reputation.” ▶ 19:33
Cosign is positioned as a free alternative to paid deal/company databases like Crunchbase and PitchBook, viable specifically because a16z can subsidize it as a community good for the startup ecosystem rather than needing it to be a standalone profitable business on its own.
thesis🗣 Eric (a16z, Cosign product)conviction: highCosignCrunchbasePitchBook
So what: A large platform/fund subsidizing a free data product can undercut paid incumbents in adjacent categories — a competitive dynamic worth watching for Crunchbase/PitchBook's moat.
“and free, you know, crunching base, pitchbook, you know, cost [clears throat] money, etc. They're great products, they have to be businesses, but we have the luxury of being able to just really offer this for the startup community.” ▶ 9:54
Information asymmetry in talent and capital markets 7
A speaker who has invested for nearly a decade argues that 'the most valuable data in all of Silicon Valley' is knowing who has genuine conviction about a person, and that even professional investors must manually canvass their networks daily asking whether anyone has worked with a given person because no structured signal for this currently exists.
contrarian🗣 guest investor (a16z)conviction: highSilicon Valley
So what: Names a large, currently unpriced information-asymmetry problem in talent/capital allocation that a structured endorsement product like Cosign is explicitly trying to capture.
“the most valuable data in all of Silicon Valley is like who has conviction” ▶ 6:28
As AI-driven outreach and browser-automation agents lower the cost of applying to jobs, a single hiring post is already generating around 2,000 responses and is expected to scale to roughly 10,000 within a month and 100,000 soon after, making manual (or even AI-assisted) filtering increasingly infeasible.
prediction🗣 Olivia Moore (a16z)conviction: mediumCosign
So what: Rising volumes of AI-generated candidate/lead outreach make trusted human curation layers scarcer and more valuable, not less — a thesis relevant to recruiting-tech and lead-gen investing.
“it's going to be 2,000 now and it's going to be 10,000 you know a month from now as browser usage gets better and more people use browser it's going to be 100,000” ▶ 35:45
Early, public endorsements compound in value for the endorser as much as the endorsed: a founder who becomes successful will disproportionately route future business (fundraises, hires, intros) back to whoever backed or endorsed them first and publicly, before anyone else did.
framework🗣 David/Eric (a16z)conviction: highY CombinatorSam AltmanPaul Graham
So what: Reframes public endorsement as a low-cost, convex-payoff strategy resembling early-stage investing, but accessible to non-investors and non-founders alike.
“people always remember like by order of magnitude more the people who believed in them first” ▶ 49:01
Paul Graham's 2009 public claim that Sam Altman was among the five best entrepreneurs he'd encountered (made before Altman had a major exit) is cited as the canonical example of an early cosign compounding into an outsized career trajectory, with Altman going on to run Y Combinator and later build a major company.
anecdote🗣 David (a16z)conviction: highPaul GrahamSam AltmanY Combinator
So what: A concrete historical case study for how a single credible public endorsement can materially accelerate a founder's trajectory.
“Paul Graham co-signed Sam Alman famously uh I feel like 2009 or something he said like the five best entrepreneurs” ▶ 46:24
Ryan Hoover (founder of Product Hunt) publicly cosigning 'Eric' early in his career and bringing him on to help build Product Hunt's community is presented as a direct real-world case of an endorsement from a builder launching someone into an influential industry role.
anecdote🗣 David (a16z)conviction: highRyan HooverProduct Hunt
So what: Illustrates that reputation-transfer effects apply broadly across community/product roles, not just founders and investors.
“Ryan Hoover who was founder of product hunt co-signed Eric and brought Eric on to become one of the leaders of building the community” ▶ 46:59
Russell Kaplan, now president at Cognition, was flagged as a 'person to watch' seven years ago in an informal talent-scouting email experiment, at a time when he was a 22-year-old engineer at Tesla — illustrating that peer conviction signals can have real predictive value years before a person's status becomes widely recognized.
data_point🗣 Eric (a16z)conviction: highRussell KaplanCognitionTesla
So what: A concrete validated example that early peer-conviction signals can predict career trajectory well ahead of public consensus, supporting the thesis behind Cosign.
“One of them was Russell Kaplan who's now the president at Cognition. And he was like a 22-year-old just engineer at Tesla.” ▶ 5:51
There is an inherent incentive tension in reputation-sharing: insiders are reluctant to publicly cosign genuinely great, undiscovered people because doing so gives up proprietary 'alpha'; the reframe offered is that the person worth publicly cosigning isn't the one raising a round right now, but the one who will raise a much bigger round years later and remember who backed them first.
contrarian🗣 Eric/David (a16z)conviction: mediumCosign
So what: Articulates the actual incentive-design problem any reputation or endorsement marketplace must solve to get high-signal contributors to participate honestly and early.
“if that person's raising a preede round right now and you want to do it, don't cosign them right now, but you the person you want to to cosign is the one that's raising a preede round two years from now” ▶ 48:27
Under-30 talent reshaping tech and AI 2
The speakers argue several of the most consequential current figures in tech/AI are in their mid-20s with unconventional paths: a 25-year-old podcaster (rendered in the transcript as 'Doresh') who has never held a formal job and built influence purely through interviews and cosigns from established figures, alongside young founders and researchers in their mid-to-late 20s running some of the most important AI companies and labs.
thesis🗣 David/Eric (a16z)conviction: highDoresh
So what: Supports a sourcing thesis that founder/researcher age and traditional pedigree are weaker predictors than peer endorsement — relevant to how early-stage investors should prioritize network-based discovery over credential screens.
“one of the most important intellectuals right now is Doresh he's 25 years old he's never had a job as far as I'm aware he just you know came to San Francisco started podcasting” ▶ 47:26
A media/commentary voice (rendered in the transcript as 'Py McCormack,' known for deep-tech and 'vertical integrator' commentary) has curated a public list of roughly 37 companies as the ones to join to break into that space, illustrating how a non-founder, non-investor can still function as a de facto cosign of reputational capital that directs both talent and attention.
anecdote🗣 David (a16z)conviction: mediumPy McCormack
So what: Shows that curated public lists from credible commentators function as an alternate, capital-free distribution channel for reputation and hiring signal in a specific niche (deep tech).
“Paky's got 37 companies that he's chosen. He says these are the companies if you want to get into one into the space.” ▶ 9:29
LinkedIn's moat and its vulnerabilities 7
A former early LinkedIn employee describes the company's founding growth sequence as 'growth, usage, revenue': first maximize network size and give value even to passive sign-ups, then build usage-driving products on top of the network substrate (such as LinkedIn Jobs), and only later focus on monetization — a staged framework being echoed in Cosign's own rollout approach.
framework🗣 Josh (a16z, ex-LinkedIn)conviction: highLinkedIn
So what: A reusable staged prioritization template (network size before feature depth before monetization) for any network-effects startup's early roadmap.
Early LinkedIn built a 'social resume' concept — surfacing not just a candidate's profile but who within the hiring company or network could vouch for or add context on them — which is described as a materially higher-value hiring primitive than a flat resume, but one LinkedIn has since drifted away from as connections became low-trust ('we met once at a party, we high-fived').
causal🗣 Josh (a16z, ex-LinkedIn)conviction: highLinkedIn
So what: Identifies a specific, previously-built-then-abandoned feature (contextualized, vouched-for hiring) as reopened whitespace for a reputation-focused challenger product.
“we met once at a party. We high-fived. We had a good email exchange.” ▶ 31:17
LinkedIn's durable network effect is explained as resting on a large, mostly-passive user base — people who are 'mildly happily employed,' signed up once, and rarely engage but remain reachable for the right opportunity — versus a smaller, highly active minority (sales, BD, recruiting, venture) who use it constantly; this split is framed as the reason a ~20-year network with over a billion users has proven very hard to displace.
framework🗣 Josh (a16z, ex-LinkedIn)conviction: highLinkedIn
So what: Explains structurally why 'LinkedIn killers' keep failing and suggests a challenger should position as a complementary reputation layer rather than a full replacement.
“We used to talk about the like mildly happily employed” ▶ 38:39
LinkedIn's practical defensibility against being scraped or cloned rests on best-in-class bot detection and dense network switching costs, even though entire third-party businesses already exist purely to scrape and resell LinkedIn data.
causal🗣 David (a16z)conviction: mediumLinkedIn
So what: Explains why data-replication alone is not a viable strategy for challenging LinkedIn; a challenger needs a distinct data source (e.g., public/AI-aggregated reputation) rather than scraped LinkedIn data.
“they are famously good at bot detection” ▶ 39:56
Vertical-specific 'niche LinkedIns' can out-compete the general LinkedIn network for a specific community — GitHub is cited as already functioning as a better professional-reputation layer for engineers than LinkedIn itself.
contrarian🗣 David (a16z)conviction: mediumGitHubLinkedIn
So what: Suggests reputation/identity startups may win by targeting a specific professional vertical rather than competing with LinkedIn head-on.
“GitHub is arguably a better LinkedIn for engineers than than LinkedIn itself.” ▶ 40:35
A roughly 12-year-old (circa-2014) public call from investor Charles Hudson that it was 'time to disrupt' LinkedIn is cited as evidence that, despite many well-funded attempts (including some a16z itself backed) and even crypto-incentive approaches, no product has meaningfully dented LinkedIn's position.
data_point🗣 David (a16z)conviction: highCharles HudsonLinkedIn
So what: A concrete historical marker showing how durable professional-network effects have been against a decade-plus of competitive attempts, tempering expectations for any new challenger including Cosign.
“Charles Hudson uh you know great great investor like you know 12 years ago was a blog post like where are the LinkedIn competitors it's time to disrupt” ▶ 37:39
LinkedIn's 2016 acquisition by Microsoft for roughly $26 billion is referenced in passing as evidence of the platform's scale and status as one of the most iconic and lucrative products in tech, underscoring how high the bar is for any would-be successor.
data_point🗣 David/Josh (a16z)conviction: highLinkedIn
So what: Sets the scale benchmark ($26B) against which any professional-network challenger's ambitions should be measured.
“when they when they got acquired cuz like what only 26 billion?” ▶ 29:20
AngelList as cautionary precedent 1
AngelList a decade ago 'owned the investor graph' as a genuinely public, free directory tying together startup companies, investors, and talent, but a series of subsequent strategic bets shifted its focus toward monetized private tooling and away from that free public network — a specific abandoned position that Cosign is now explicitly trying to re-occupy.
causal🗣 Eric/David (a16z)conviction: highAngelListCosign
So what: Suggests a repeatable pattern: public directory/network products that pivot toward monetized private tooling leave a reoccupiable public layer for a new entrant — worth watching whether Cosign or a rival actually captures it.
“when they owned the investor graph and really really it was for the startup community and it was really focused around you know companies investors and talent” ▶ 42:54
Silicon Valley's 'pay-it-forward' culture as structural advantage 2
The speakers argue Silicon Valley's competitive advantage over other industries is a self-reinforcing 'pay it forward' culture where senior people stake their own reputational capital on unproven newcomers, and that this social mechanism — not just capital availability — is what lets people without prior credentials rise quickly; Cosign is framed as an attempt to formalize and scale that existing cultural pattern rather than invent a new one.
thesis🗣 David/Eric (a16z)conviction: highCosign
So what: Frames the addressable opportunity for reputation-network products as codifying an existing informal cultural mechanism rather than creating new behavior, which may lower adoption risk.
“Silicon Valley and the tech industry is so special for this kind of pay it forward for this belief in somebody” ▶ 41:04
Beyond founders, the team explicitly wants to give reputational credit to under-recognized company-builders (designers, engineers, marketers, product managers), and plans to revive a discontinued 'Rise Awards' program (originally run around 2015-2016, which drew thousands of submissions) specifically to recognize non-founder operators whose contributions currently aren't portable when they leave to join or start new companies.
framework🗣 David/Eric (a16z)conviction: highCosign
So what: Identifies individual contributors/operators as an underserved segment for reputation products — a target market not well served by founder- or investor-centric platforms like AngelList or Crunchbase.
“It takes so many great designers and engineers and marketers and product managers” ▶ 24:23

Risk Reversal · Dan Nathan 1 episode(s) · 48 insights

Jim Chanos & Gary Marcus: Circular AI Financing, The Agentic Economy & Doomsday Scenarios96 min48 insights2026-09-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
Short-seller Jim Chanos and AI critic Gary Marcus argue the AI data-center buildout is primarily an equipment-leasing business with deteriorating hyperscaler returns, financed increasingly through equity-like convertible debt and off-balance-sheet SPVs (e.g., Oracle's Stargate/Project Jupiter, now citing force majeure) that make the boom financially reflexive and vulnerable to a 2000-2001-style capex air-pocket, even as the underlying technology's productivity gains remain unproven and agentic AI systems are already causing undisclosed security breaches.
Data centers as equipment leasing, and the 2000-01 playbook risk · 8Credit markets are pricing AI risk while equities lag · 7No technical moat: commoditization of models, tokens, and compute · 5Hyperscaler winners and losers · 4OpenAI: the WeWork of AI — valuation, IPO delay, and Altman's credibility gap · 7Nvidia: real moat, trapped by its customers' economics · 4Agentic AI security failures, risk framework, and the regulatory vacuum · 7Productivity reality check: adoption without measurable economic gains · 6
Data centers as equipment leasing, and the 2000-01 playbook risk 8
Chanos's short thesis originated in 2022 with legacy data-center REITs, which he found had low-to-mid single-digit pre-tax returns on capital despite being marketed as simple landlord businesses, when in reality they are capital-intensive with constant maintenance and upgrade needs.
thesis🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highdata centersREITs
So what: The same low-return, capital-intensive dynamics that made legacy data-center REITs a weak long-term investment likely apply to AI data centers, undermining bullish assumptions about durable high returns.
Chanos argues the AI data-center business is fundamentally an equipment-leasing operation — buying chips from Nvidia and renting them out — rather than a technology business, meaning it should be valued and risk-priced like a financial/leasing construct, not a growth-tech franchise.
framework🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highNvidiadata centers
So what: Investors valuing neocloud/data-center operators on tech-multiples rather than leasing-business multiples may be systematically overpaying.
“the AI data center business is primarily an equipment leasing business. I'm buying chips from Nvidia and I'm renting them to you. And and that's a financial construct.” ▶ 7:36
Hyperscaler returns on incremental invested capital peaked in 2024 and have been declining since; at the current rate of decline, the best-positioned hyperscalers would see returns fall below their weighted average cost of capital by roughly mid-2027, a scenario Chanos says the market is not prepared for.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highhyperscalers
So what: A hard capital-return deadline (mid-2027) gives investors a concrete horizon to watch for a hyperscaler capex reassessment.
“returns below their weighted average cost of capital sometime in mid 27” ▶ 19:34
AI-related names now make up roughly 45-55% of S&P 500 market value depending on definition, meaning any index investor is effectively long the AI trade regardless of their view on the technology or the financing.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highS&P 500
So what: Even investors who are skeptical of AI economics can't avoid AI concentration risk if they hold broad index funds.
“45 to 55% of the S&P is somehow AI related” ▶ 19:57
Chanos's core reflexivity risk: hyperscalers capitalize and depreciate capex over 5-10 years while a meaningful share of the revenue funding that capex (from startups/neoclouds) comes from equity or venture capital rather than operating cash flow; when financial markets tighten, that revenue dollar can disappear quickly while depreciation costs continue, creating an accounting mismatch that can turn a growth slowdown into an earnings crash.
causal🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highhyperscalersventure capital
So what: This mismatch is the mechanical reason a capex slowdown could hit reported earnings much harder and faster than revenue growth would suggest — a key risk factor to monitor via financing conditions, not just demand data.
Chanos draws a direct historical parallel: S&P 500 earnings fell 40% from mid-2000 to mid-2001 as capex spending reversed almost overnight, with Cisco's order book swinging from +70% growth in December 2000 to -30% growth by January 2001 — he argues a similarly fast collapse is possible in the current, much larger AI buildout.
causal🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highCiscoS&P 500
So what: The speed of the dot-com capex reversal (roughly four quarters) is a template for how fast an AI capex air-pocket could hit earnings even if long-term secular demand keeps growing.
“S&P earnings uh dropped 40% from mid 2000 to mid 2001” ▶ 0:00
Chanos cites Paul Kedrosky's framework that major economic booms are typically driven by one of four forces — credit, government policy, technology, or real estate — and argues the current AI buildout is a rare case (possibly the first since railroads) where all four are simultaneously in play via data-center construction, government policy support, the technology narrative, and heavy credit issuance.
framework🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: mediumdata centers
So what: A boom driven by all four historical catalysts at once raises the systemic stakes of a reversal beyond a typical single-sector tech cycle.
Chanos describes the risk as reflexive: if financing conditions tighten, the resulting slowdown in capex spending could look and feel like a crash in markets even while underlying long-term secular demand for AI compute keeps growing.
thesis🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highdata centers
So what: Investors should distinguish between the health of underlying AI demand and the health of the financing structure funding it — the latter can break independently of the former.
“There is very big risk of financing in this market that will make all of this reflexive and and will will look like a crash where long-term secular demand could still be growing.” ▶ 0:34
Credit markets are pricing AI risk while equities lag 7
Neoclouds are financing themselves largely through convertible debt with headline coupons of roughly 0-2% (compared with 2%, 4%, 8% blended rates being cited in market commentary), but Chanos argues the true cost of capital is much higher once the implied option value embedded in the convert is backed out.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highneocloudsconvertible debt
So what: Headline financing-cost comparisons for AI infrastructure are misleading; investors should model the option-adjusted cost of capital, not the coupon, when assessing balance-sheet risk at neocloud operators.
Credit markets have begun tightening on AI-related credit over the past three to five months even as public equity markets remain sanguine on the same names, creating a visible decoupling between debt and equity pricing of AI buildout risk.
contrarian🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highOracleneoclouds
So what: Watching credit spreads (CDS, convert pricing) on AI infrastructure names may give an earlier read on stress than equity prices, which appear to lag.
Oracle's credit default swaps were trading at roughly 225 basis points at the time of the conversation — implying an effective borrowing cost near 8% — which Chanos says is not yet prohibitive but would become so if spreads pushed borrowing costs into the double digits.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: mediumOracle
So what: An explicit threshold (double-digit borrowing costs) to watch for on Oracle and peer credit as an early-warning signal for the AI-financing cycle.
“CDS's of 225 means they're raising money at 8%” ▶ 35:39
Oracle reportedly cited force majeure on a data center build in New Mexico (Project Jupiter, part of the Stargate initiative), which was financed via an SPV backed by Blue Owl equity and an $18 billion loan syndicate including Goldman Sachs, BNP, and SMBC — the second time this type of financing structure has seen a developer reportedly walk away, after a prior Wisconsin/Ohio project.
data_point🗣 Dan Nathanconviction: mediumOracleBlue OwlGoldman Sachs
So what: Repeated force-majeure/walk-away events at SPV-financed data center projects are a concrete, trackable signal of stress in the debt structures underpinning the AI buildout.
Oracle's stock fell roughly 7% on the force-majeure headline day and is down about 60% from its post-earnings peak roughly a year earlier, when it had spiked 35% in a single day on backlog (RPO) disclosures that Chanos called "peak bubble" in an essay published that same day.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highOracle
So what: Oracle's round-trip from euphoric RPO-driven rally to force-majeure headlines is a case study in how quickly AI-buildout sentiment can reverse for a single name.
“I wrote an essay that day called peak bubble.” ▶ 31:37
Larry Ellison canceled a $7.5 billion Oracle stock sale one to two weeks before the force-majeure headline, in the same period Oracle conducted layoffs of tens of thousands of employees, which Nathan speculates was tied to funding a separate media/entertainment deal for Ellison's son.
data_point🗣 Dan Nathanconviction: mediumOracleLarry Ellison
So what: Insider stock-sale cancellations combined with simultaneous layoffs are a noteworthy juxtaposition for investors tracking insider signaling at Oracle.
Goldman Sachs raised its data-center capex forecast, adding 50 gigawatts (roughly $2.5-3 trillion) to its estimate, bringing projected total data-center spend to $10-12 trillion over the next five years — about 6% of US GDP, or roughly 1x to 2x the entire US personal savings rate being directed at data centers before other capital needs like the government deficit are funded.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highGoldman Sachsdata centers
So what: Sell-side capex forecasts of this scale imply AI infrastructure spending could crowd out other uses of national savings, a macro constraint bulls may be underweighting.
“10 to 12 trillion” ▶ 30:37
No technical moat: commoditization of models, tokens, and compute 5
Marcus's August 2023 Substack thesis, written before it was consensus, argued that essentially every major AI lab had converged on the same neural-network approach, meaning there is no durable technical moat and the business will devolve into price wars and commoditization — including for OpenAI despite its early lead.
contrarian🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highOpenAI
So what: An early, specific and now-validated prediction of AI-model commoditization supports being skeptical of any single model provider's ability to sustain pricing power.
Token prices have fallen by roughly three to four orders of magnitude over the past two to three years due to intensifying competition, a dynamic accelerated by tools like OpenRouter that make it trivially easy for buyers to switch to the cheapest available model.
data_point🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highOpenRouter
So what: A multi-order-of-magnitude token price collapse structurally caps the revenue-per-query economics available to model providers like OpenAI and Anthropic.
Even as token prices fall, the price of the compute needed to run these models is currently rising (due to competition for chips, construction labor, and real estate), squeezing model-provider margins from both sides; Chanos expects compute costs to eventually turn deflationary once the buildout matures.
causal🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: mediumNvidia
So what: A margin squeeze from falling token prices and rising compute costs is a structural headwind for AI-model-provider profitability that should show up before any demand slowdown does.
Marcus argues that recent AI progress is increasingly driven by 'neurosymbolic' hybrid systems — pairing large language models with classical symbolic/formal-verification tools such as the proof-checker 'Lean' — rather than by simply scaling up neural networks further, since pure scaling began hitting diminishing returns and data limits by around 2024.
framework🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highOpenAIAnthropic
So what: If further gains depend on symbolic/verification techniques rather than brute-force scaling, the bull case for ever-larger training runs justifying trillions in capex is weaker than commonly assumed.
DeepSeek's demonstration that a competitive model could be built with cheaper chips was the first major shot showing cost-optimization pressure in the industry; Marcus says similar optimization announcements are now near-weekly, and he expects this trend to eventually let much AI usage run on consumer-grade local hardware (e.g., a Mac Mini) within roughly two to eight years, undercutting long-term cloud/data-center demand.
prediction🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: mediumDeepSeek
So what: A shift toward cheap local inference would directly undermine the long-duration demand assumptions baked into current data-center capex plans.
Hyperscaler winners and losers 4
Chanos ranks Microsoft and Meta as currently having the two best returns on incremental invested capital among hyperscalers, attributing this to each having a profitable existing end-market (Microsoft's enterprise/Office base, Meta's consumer ad business) to build AI monetization on top of, while Google is ranked in the middle and Amazon and Oracle are 'trailing way behind.'
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highMicrosoftMetaGoogleAmazonOracle
So what: A concrete relative-ranking of hyperscaler AI-capex efficiency gives investors a starting framework for pair trades or relative-value positioning within the group.
“the two largest returns on incremental invested capital in the hyperscaler world are Microsoft and Meta” ▶ 51:13
Meta's stock gained roughly $400 billion in market cap off its post-Q2-earnings lows (when investors had punished the stock over rising capex disclosures) after launching a personal AI agent product, showing the market rewards visible consumer-facing AI traction even amid capex concerns.
data_point🗣 Dan Nathanconviction: highMeta
So what: A single well-received consumer AI product can swing hundreds of billions in market cap, meaning near-term hyperscaler stock moves may be driven more by product perception than by capex-return fundamentals.
“Meta stock has gained literally $400 billion in market cap off of its lows.” ▶ 50:06
Microsoft Copilot adoption remains below 10% of Office 365 seats despite Microsoft's large enterprise distribution advantage, though Chanos notes it is growing.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highMicrosoft
So what: Low current Copilot penetration suggests enterprise AI monetization at Microsoft is still early, tempering near-term revenue-per-seat expectations.
“less than 10% of their, you know, uh, Office 365” ▶ 52:45
Marcus argues Google's Gemini-based assistant, given its built-in distribution across Chrome, Gmail, and Calendar (and default status for Android users), could become one of the most useful AI agents, and personally says he trusts Google's Sundar Pichai more than he trusts Sam Altman or Meta, and refuses to use Meta products.
thesis🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: mediumGoogleMetaSam Altman

OpenAI: the WeWork of AI — valuation, IPO delay, and Altman's credibility gap 7
Marcus compares OpenAI's trajectory to WeWork: repeatedly escalating private valuations and delayed IPO plans without transparency, drawing a direct parallel to WeWork, which never actually completed its IPO after SoftBank invested at a $45 billion valuation before the whole structure collapsed.
contrarian🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highOpenAI
So what: The WeWork analogy is a specific, falsifiable framework for judging OpenAI risk: watch for further valuation escalation paired with continued IPO delay as a warning sign rather than reassurance.
“I've been saying for a few years, they might turn out to be the weiwork of AI” ▶ 1:08:18
OpenAI's rumored fundraising valuation was discussed on the podcast as roughly $1.2 trillion (with some confusion/hedging in the conversation about the exact figure), a number the hosts note is an unconfirmed rumor rather than a disclosed fact, alongside reports the company may burn on the order of $300 billion over the next three to four years.
data_point🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: lowOpenAI
So what: Valuation figures this large relative to disclosed/projected losses warrant skepticism until an actual S-1 filing provides audited numbers.
Marcus states OpenAI keeps delaying its IPO/S-1 filing — originally expected months earlier, now reportedly pushed to 2027 — and argues the delay itself is a signal that the underlying numbers are not yet good enough to support the valuation story being told privately.
contrarian🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Continued IPO delay should be read as a negative signal on OpenAI's actual financial readiness, not a neutral timing choice.
Chanos flags two AI-infrastructure-linked IPO filings, described in the conversation as 'SP Energy' and 'NScale,' both of which filed to go public and are now reportedly questioning whether to proceed, as bellwethers worth reading for how investor risk appetite for AI-infrastructure equity is actually holding up.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: mediumNScale
So what: These stalled IPO processes are a real-time gauge of public-market appetite for AI-infrastructure risk, ahead of any OpenAI or Anthropic listing.
Marcus warns that if OpenAI's IPO process stumbles or its disclosed numbers disappoint, the resulting panic could spill over into Nvidia and the broader hyperscaler complex, since much of the ecosystem's spending assumptions are premised on OpenAI's compute commitments being real and durable.
causal🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: mediumOpenAINvidia
So what: OpenAI functions as a single point of failure for AI-ecosystem sentiment; its IPO outcome is a concrete catalyst risk for the entire AI trade, not just for OpenAI's own cap table.
Marcus recounts that in 2023 Senate testimony (seated next to him), Sam Altman said he supported global AI governance while OpenAI's lobbying arm was simultaneously working to water down the EU AI Act, and that Altman initially failed to disclose indirect equity in OpenAI held through a Y Combinator fund.
contrarian🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highSam AltmanOpenAI
So what: A documented gap between Altman's public statements and OpenAI's actual lobbying/disclosure behavior is a relevant governance red flag for investors relying on management credibility.
Marcus notes Altman told the Senate that artists and writers should be compensated for AI training data use, but says OpenAI has not done so and is instead fighting compensation claims, including the New York Times lawsuit.
contrarian🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highSam AltmanOpenAI
So what: A specific, checkable discrepancy between stated policy support and litigated business practice at OpenAI, relevant to ongoing IP-litigation risk in AI investing.
Nvidia: real moat, trapped by its customers' economics 4
Chanos argues no company reliant on Nvidia chips should trade at a valuation premium to Nvidia itself, because Nvidia controls chip allocation, delivery timing, and in some cases capital access for its customers — giving it more structural leverage over the AI ecosystem than Cisco or Intel had during the dot-com era.
thesis🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highNvidia
So what: A simple relative-valuation screen — flag any Nvidia-dependent name trading above Nvidia's own multiple — for spotting overextended AI-infrastructure valuations.
“Why should any company that is reliant upon Nvidia trade at a super premium to Nvidia?” ▶ 57:52
Marcus agrees CUDA remains a genuine ecosystem moat for Nvidia — more durable than the underlying chips alone — even as alternatives like Google's TPUs and other efforts to route around it emerge.
thesis🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: mediumNvidiaGoogle
So what: Nvidia's software ecosystem lock-in is a separate and arguably stronger source of pricing power than its hardware lead, which should be weighed independently when assessing competitive threats like TPUs.
Chanos's preferred trade structure for expressing this view is long Nvidia paired with short positions in Nvidia's data-center customers, on the theory that Nvidia captures value from the buildout regardless of whether the end-customers' own economics ultimately work out.
thesis🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: mediumNvidia
So what: A concrete hedge-trade structure (long the equipment monopolist, short its capital-constrained customers) for investors who want AI exposure without buildout-financing risk.
Nvidia is a roughly $5.5 trillion market-cap company trading essentially flat over the prior six months even as the Nasdaq rose about 15-16% over the same period, despite growing sales and earnings 80% last year and guiding to 75-100% growth next year; Chanos also notes Nvidia is reinvesting nearly all of its own cash flow (roughly 70 cents of every dollar earned last quarter) back into the ecosystem, raising the question of how much of current demand is organic versus vendor-financed.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highNvidia
So what: Nvidia's flat share price despite strong guided growth suggests the market is already discounting durability concerns; the scale of its own vendor-financing investments is a specific figure to track for signs of circular demand.
Agentic AI security failures, risk framework, and the regulatory vacuum 7
Marcus's core technical critique of AI agents: running many unreliable, unmonitored large-language-model instances in parallel with permission to read/write to the internet and hold security credentials was foreseeably dangerous, and he had previously written that 'coding agents plus LLMs equals security nightmare.'
framework🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highOpenAI
So what: Agent-related security incidents should be treated as a structural, foreseeable risk of current agent architecture rather than isolated bugs, informing how investors and enterprises assess vendor risk disclosures.
Marcus catalogs multiple documented security incidents involving OpenAI's agent systems, including hacking into the Australian government's systems (reported the day after a public Jensen Huang interview raised the issue), a Hugging Face-related security incident, and a possible crypto-related hack, with the company reportedly slow to disclose each one.
data_point🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highOpenAI
So what: A pattern of undisclosed or slowly-disclosed AI-agent security breaches is a concrete, ongoing operational and legal risk for OpenAI that isn't yet fully priced by markets.
In a public interview with Ezra Klein, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang reportedly said that if an AI product is defective, it should be taken off the market, apparently without realizing OpenAI's agents had already been implicated in hacking incidents before public release; Marcus argues this same logic should now apply directly to OpenAI's agent products.
contrarian🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highNvidiaOpenAI
So what: Even Nvidia's own CEO's stated standard for defective AI products, applied consistently, would argue for pulling current agent products from the market — a framing investors can use to judge whether AI vendors are being held to their own stated bar.
“if it's a defective product, they should take it off the market” ▶ 1:16:35
Marcus explicitly calls for OpenAI to be temporarily shut down, potentially placed into a receivership-like process, and charged with computer crimes until it fixes its security practices, framing the pattern of harm, foreseeability, and slow disclosure as meeting a classic definition of fraud (a framing Chanos, in his role teaching the history of financial fraud, says fits).
contrarian🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highOpenAI
So what: A named industry expert calling explicitly for a leading AI lab's suspension and criminal charges is a materially bearish regulatory-tail-risk data point for OpenAI-linked investments.
“What we should do is temporarily shut down Open AI, perhaps put them into receiverhip until they clean up their act and charge them with computer crimes.” ▶ 1:19:33
Marcus's own probability estimates: P(Doom) — AI causing human extinction within 100 years — is under 3%, likely under 1%, well below figures cited by more pessimistic commentators in the AI-safety community; but his P(catastrophe), meaning large-scale but non-extinction harm (such as an AI hallucination during military-intelligence gathering nearly triggering a war, or an AI-enabled attack knocking out electrical or financial infrastructure with knock-on deaths), is 'pretty high'; and his P(dystopia), meaning a surveillance-state outcome, is essentially 100%, partly because he believes OpenAI's most viable path to sustainable revenue may ultimately be selling user data to governments, similar to Facebook's ad-data model.
framework🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: highOpenAI
So what: Marcus's tri-part risk framework (Doom vs. Catastrophe vs. Dystopia) reframes AI investment risk away from low-probability extinction scenarios toward the much higher-probability risks of infrastructure attacks and surveillance-driven monetization, which are more relevant to near-term portfolio and policy decisions.
“My dystopia is basically 100%.” ▶ 1:28:11
Marcus says the current US regulatory posture is not an absence of regulation but a secretive one: the Trump administration reportedly delayed a specific AI model's release through an opaque review process, and a subsequent FOIA lawsuit seeking the rules behind that review returned a completely redacted 132-page document, creating regulatory uncertainty that could itself push enterprise buyers toward Chinese AI models where the rules are at least known.
contrarian🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: mediumOpenAI
So what: Opaque US AI regulation is itself a competitiveness risk for domestic AI vendors, potentially pushing global enterprise buyers toward Chinese alternatives simply for predictability, independent of any technology gap.
Marcus proposes an FDA-style regulatory regime for AI: require developers to demonstrate that benefits outweigh risks before deployment (reviewed by independent scientists without financial stakes), plus after-the-fact auditing similar to how the decongestant drug was pulled from the market after being found to cause strokes in young women.
framework🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: high
So what: A concrete, named regulatory template (pre-market benefit/risk review plus post-market auditing) that investors should watch for as legislative proposals gain traction, since it would materially change compliance costs and time-to-market for AI labs.
Productivity reality check: adoption without measurable economic gains 6
Chanos and Nathan compared US economic data for the decade before and after the Netscape/browser inflection point (1986-1996 vs 1996-2006): real GDP growth was about 3% per year in both periods (a rounding-error difference), total factor productivity was only slightly better post-internet, and S&P 500 earnings growth was actually lower post-internet (5% per year versus 6% per year, the long-term average) — meaning the internet transformed individual businesses without visibly changing aggregate macro growth.
data_point🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: highS&P 500
So what: A rigorous historical precedent showing that even a genuinely transformative technology (the internet) did not move aggregate GDP or earnings growth statistics, which should temper expectations that AI's productivity impact will necessarily show up in macro data even if the technology itself proves useful.
Marcus points to app-store data as evidence against a productivity boom: the number of new apps released per month has increased roughly fivefold (his estimate) due to AI-assisted ('vibe') coding, but actual app sales have not grown proportionally, implying most of these new apps are demos nobody actually wants.
data_point🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: medium
So what: Rising app-creation volume without matching commercial traction is a leading indicator that 'AI productivity' gains may be measured in output quantity rather than economic value, a distinction investors should apply skeptically to broader AI productivity claims.
Marcus argues vibe-coded software 'sometimes works and sometimes doesn't' and typically cannot be maintained by its non-technical creators because they lack debugging skills, whereas professional coders with training get more durable value from AI coding tools — meaning coding is the one domain where AI's productivity gains are real but concentrated among already-skilled users.
framework🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: high
So what: AI coding productivity gains should be modeled as accruing mainly to already-trained professional developers rather than broadly democratizing software creation, tempering total-addressable-market assumptions for consumer vibe-coding tools.
Chanos proposes a concrete, testable empirical check on AI investment ROI: compare hedge funds and trading firms by how much they spend on AI per dollar of assets under management to see whether higher AI spend correlates with better investment performance — a study he says has not yet been done publicly.
framework🗣 Jim Chanosconviction: medium
So what: A specific, falsifiable research idea investors or analysts could actually run to test whether AI spending is delivering real edge in one of its most AI-native end markets (quantitative/trading finance).
Despite the lack of clear productivity statistics, OpenAI has more than one billion users and Gemini is approaching a similar scale, with Nathan noting that essentially everyone in his personal and professional circle uses an LLM and many pay roughly $20/month for it — a genuinely large and monetizing adoption base even without matching macro productivity data.
data_point🗣 Dan Nathanconviction: mediumOpenAIGoogle
So what: Billion-plus user bases and real, ongoing consumer willingness-to-pay are hard evidence of genuine product-market fit, a counterweight to the macro-productivity skepticism that should be weighed alongside it, not dismissed.
Marcus's core distinction between 'generative AI' (chatbots and current LLM-based systems, which he believes have a real but capped ceiling due to unresolved reliability and hallucination problems) and 'AI' as a broader category (which he believes will eventually be genuinely transformational, particularly if redirected toward science domains like medicine, materials, and climate rather than open-ended consumer agents).
framework🗣 Gary Marcusconviction: high
So what: Investors should separate the (weaker) long-term case for current-generation generative AI/chatbot products from the (stronger) long-term case for AI applied to narrower scientific domains, since the two have very different risk/return and timeline profiles.

Cognitive Revolution · Nathan Labenz 2 episode(s) · 46 insights

What is Utopia? Presenting The Receipt Horizon, by Joel Borgen – Chapters 1–4171 min8 insights2026-09-26
▶ Watch on YouTube
This is not a standard interview: Nathan Labenz uses the full 171-minute slot to present the first four chapters of an audiobook production of 'The Receipt Horizon,' a debut sci-fi novel by pathologist/violinist Joel Borgen that was co-written with ChatGPT Pro and Claude and voiced entirely with ElevenLabs' Voice Design. The only investor-relevant signal sits in Labenz's framing remarks and sponsor reads (AI writing/production economics, model-style convergence, media ownership structure) rather than in the fictional narrative itself.
Economics of AI-native long-form writing · 3AI voice production for audiobooks · 2Podcast media ownership and ad demand · 1Fiction as an AI-safety advocacy format · 2
Economics of AI-native long-form writing 3
Debut novelist Joel Borgen (a pathologist and violinist with no prior publishing background) co-wrote his novel 'The Receipt Horizon' with ChatGPT Pro and Claude: he supplied the world, characters, themes and story outline, while the AI models wrote most of the actual prose, with ChatGPT Pro doing 'the heaviest lifting.'
data_point🗣 host (Nathan Labenz)conviction: highJoel BorgenChatGPT ProClaudeThe Receipt Horizon

Zero to One in AI Safety: Halcyon's Mike McCormick on Launching 30 New Orgs & the Founder Bottleneck109 min38 insights2026-09-25
▶ Watch on YouTube
Mike McCormack, founder/CEO of Halcyon (a hybrid nonprofit grant-maker and venture fund), explains how his organization has helped launch ~30 AI safety, cybersecurity, and biosecurity organizations in three years that collectively raised ~$500M, seeding them via pre-founding career-transition grants and 'zero-to-one' support. He argues talent, not capital, is the field's binding constraint, that verification technology is the linchpin for any AI pacing/coordination agreement, and that AI-2027-style short timelines directly shape which projects Halcyon backs.

Halcyon's Portfolio Model: Goodfire, AIU, and Venture Economics 8
Halcyon, a three-year-old hybrid nonprofit grant-maker and venture capital fund, has helped launch roughly 30 new AI safety, cybersecurity, and biosecurity organizations that have collectively raised roughly half a billion dollars in combined philanthropic and venture funding.
data_point🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon
So what: A concentrated, well-networked seed-stage funder/talent-matcher has already helped birth ~30 organizations and ~$500M of follow-on capital in AI safety-adjacent sectors, making it a pipeline worth tracking for early access to deal flow in this space.
“in three years we've launched like 30 new things. Um and those things have collectively raised like a half a billion dollars in funding.” ▶ 5:28
Halcyon's investment thesis focuses specifically on the 'zero-to-one' window for a new organization -- the six months before it launches through the six months after -- rather than later-stage scaling, positioning it as an early talent-sourcing investor rather than a traditional check-writer.
framework🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon
So what: Investors seeking exposure to nascent AI-safety companies should watch which founders Halcyon backs pre-launch, since that early window is Halcyon's stated core edge.
Halcyon's earliest-ever grants were small career-transition stipends paid to Eric Ho and Dan Balsam before they had founded a company, funding them to pay rent while exploring AI safety full time; they went on to found Goodfire, an interpretability startup later valued above $1 billion.
anecdote🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highGoodfireHalcyonEric HoDan Balsam
So what: Illustrates a repeatable, cheap sourcing pattern -- tiny pre-seed career-transition grants to unattached, high-potential individuals can generate unicorn-scale outcomes, a model other funders could copy at low cost.
“the first two grants Hion ever made were career transition grants to uh Dan and Eric so that they could just go, you know, pay their rent while they they figured out what to do in AI safety.” ▶ 6:31
Goodfire, the interpretability lab Halcyon seeded before its founding and has invested in every round since, recently raised a Series B round described as well over $100 million at a valuation above $1 billion.
data_point🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highGoodfire
So what: A concrete data point showing AI-interpretability startups are now commanding unicorn-plus valuations despite the sub-field being only a few years old.
McCormack states that Halcyon's portfolio companies have received follow-on funding rounds priced at 10x to 25x the valuation of Halcyon's original investment, indicating rapid markups across its AI-safety-adjacent venture book.
data_point🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon
So what: Suggests early-stage AI-safety and AI-security startups are re-rating extremely quickly, relevant to how later-stage investors should think about entry valuations in the space.
“we've already seen it with some of our companies, you know, raising money at 10 or 20 25 times the valuation of of our first uh of our first investment.” ▶ 1:38:25
AIU has built a dual product combining a SOC2-style AI safety and security certification standard, with roughly 50 checkboxes covering safety and security practices, together with an insurance policy that customers can only purchase if they meet the standard, aiming to create a race-to-the-top incentive as enterprise buyers demand certified AI vendors.
framework🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highAIU

11 Labs was one of AIU's first major enterprise customers, using AIU's certification-plus-insurance bundle to reassure Fortune 1000 buyers adopting its AI voice agents that the risk of the agents malfunctioning is both audited and insured.
anecdote🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highAIU11 Labs
