“So like AIU's big customers are like growth stage AI application companies, right? So like 11 Labs I think was one of their first big customers.” ▶ 11:23
McCormack expects AIU to become a major AI risk standard but explicitly not the only one, anticipating a multi-standard ecosystem in which competing certifications become progressively harder to meet over time.
prediction🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumAIU
So what: Signals AI-risk certification and insurance is likely to become a competitive, multi-player market rather than a single monopoly standard, relevant for anyone evaluating AI-governance-as-a-service startups.
Talent, Not Capital, Is AI Safety's Binding Constraint 5
McCormack's central thesis is that experienced founders and operators, not money, have been AI safety's more important bottleneck, and he expects this imbalance to worsen as frontier-lab liquidity events unlock large new pools of philanthropic capital with too few vetted organizations to absorb it.
thesis🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon
So what: Implies capital earmarked for AI safety should prioritize funding new organization formation and founder search now, since the funding gap is closing faster than the talent gap.
“I've always thought that talent was a more important bottleneck than money in AI safety.” ▶ 53:38
McCormack predicts a near-term surge of AI-safety philanthropic capital as frontier labs approach IPOs and employee equity becomes liquid, but warns there may not be enough vetted, investable organizations ready to absorb that money responsibly.
prediction🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Signals a coming imbalance between AI-safety capital supply and deployable opportunities, favoring investment now in new-organization formation over waiting for the capital wave.
“I'm fearful of this near future moment where there's this mountain of billions and billions of dollars and not all that many great things to fund.” ▶ 54:27
McCormack argues great founders effectively solve the capital-constraint problem themselves because raising money effectively is one of the core skills of an experienced founder, reframing AI-safety funding gaps as a downstream symptom of the talent shortage rather than an independent capital-formation problem.
causal🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon
So what: Suggests philanthropic and VC dollars earmarked for AI safety are better spent on talent search and founder support than on simply writing bigger checks to existing orgs.
“one thing that experienced founders or great founders do is is raise money very effectively. So, you know, the money the money appears.” ▶ 53:49
McCormack identifies recruiting as a specific, underserved, high-leverage service category for the scaling AI-safety ecosystem, arguing organizations should lean on generalist recruiting firms -- not only mission-driven niche recruiters -- for functional roles like CFO where deep AI-safety mission alignment matters far less than raw competence.
framework🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon
So what: Points to a low-capital-intensity business opportunity -- specialized recruiting for scaling AI-safety organizations -- as the ecosystem grows headcount from dozens to hundreds of employees.
McCormack's career-transition heuristic -- 'hustle but don't rush' -- advises people moving into AI safety to spend time meeting people and learning before committing, arguing a fit that is orders of magnitude better than the median option is worth the wait versus grabbing the first available opportunity.
framework🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon
So what: A practical decision framework for career-transition timing that also reveals how Halcyon screens career-transition grant applicants -- search quality matters more than speed.
“hustle but don't rush” ▶ 40:23
AI 2027 Timeline and Verification as the Linchpin for Pacing 5
Halcyon operates on the working assumption that AI capability progress is roughly on the 'AI 2027' timeline, give or take a few months, and this belief directly determines which projects and organizations get funded, with priority given to solutions relevant within the next one to four years.
thesis🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon
So what: A well-networked AI-safety insider treats the aggressive AI-2027 capability scenario as the base case for capital allocation, implying near-term (1-4 year) AI-safety infrastructure spend should be prioritized over long-duration bets.
“we're sort of kind of on the AI 2027 timeline, uh give or take, you know, a few months, right?” ▶ 23:01
McCormack acknowledges that if a fast-takeoff via recursive self-improvement genuinely arrives within three to six months, most of Halcyon's organization-building strategy becomes moot, but says he remains hopeful that a pacing or slowdown agreement will be reached, which would extend the relevant planning horizon.
exploratory🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: lowHalcyon
So what: Flags the tail-risk scenario under which the entire 'build more AI-safety orgs' strategy fails to matter, useful for stress-testing any AI-safety investment thesis against a fast-takeoff outcome.
“I think if we truly only have three or six months to some RSI fume fast takeoff moment, then yeah, like kind of all bets are off, right?” ▶ 26:39
McCormack argues that pacing the AI frontier has shifted from a fringe position to a mainstream, Overton-window-acceptable policy stance, which he reads as a bullish signal that slowdown and verification agreements are increasingly plausible.
contrarian🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon
So what: A sentiment and regulatory-momentum data point relevant to handicapping the probability of near-term AI governance agreements such as compute or model-size caps actually being enacted.
“pacing the frontier is the new like in the Overton window normal thing to support.” ▶ 27:06
McCormack's framework holds that any AI pacing or coordination agreement -- between the US and China, between labs, or via state or federal regulation -- is only as credible as its verification mechanism, since an agreement's enforceability requires technically specific, checkable commitments such as confirming a data center is doing inference rather than training, or confirming the model actually being served matches the one that was certified.
framework🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon
So what: Implies any AI governance or regulatory deal is non-durable without a verification technology layer, making cryptographic and hardware-attestation verification startups a structurally necessary category regardless of which specific policy regime emerges.
“verification is is like the thing that's going to allow for pacing, right?” ▶ 28:22
Halcyon has invested in and helped start what McCormack describes as the first companies applying zero-knowledge proofs to AI inference verification and the first company building a fully verifiable 'neocloud,' and is currently hosting a private retreat for 25 founders spanning the cryptography, software, hardware, and governance layers of the AI verification stack.
data_point🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon
So what: Names a specific, currently underinvested infrastructure category -- cryptographic AI-inference verification and verifiable neoclouds -- that Halcyon treats as a top current priority, a concrete sourcing lead for anyone tracking AI-governance-infrastructure investment.
“we we've invested in you know like the first companies trying to use zero knowledge proofs to do inference verification we even invested and helped start uh the first company that's trying to build a fully verifiable um uh neocloud” ▶ 29:44
Biosecurity: The Most Tractable AI-Adjacent Risk Category 4
McCormack argues biosecurity is comparatively more tractable than alignment or interpretability because it is 'legible' and its general problem shape is already understood from past pandemics, unlike alignment, where he says it is unclear what fully solving the problem would even look like for superintelligent systems.
contrarian🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon
So what: Suggests capital deployed into biosecurity hardening can expect more predictable, measurable risk-reduction returns than capital deployed into alignment research, which remains conceptually undefined.
“one reason uh to be a little bit more optimistic about biocurity is that it's it's like it's legible and kind of solvable” ▶ 32:30
McCormack states that with roughly $300 million in funding, it is possible to manufacture and stockpile enough personal protective equipment to keep approximately 50 million American essential workers on the job during a future COVID-scale or worse pandemic, a concrete intervention priced out by the Halcyon-seeded organization Hrien.
data_point🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyonHrien

thesis🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon

“there are really good reasons to adopt air cleaning technology right now, even if we never had another pandemic.” ▶ 18:26
Halcyon treats field-building as an explicit sixth focus category and recently funded a new Halcyon-shaped biosecurity organization in Israel, indicating it is actively trying to replicate its own talent-sourcing and org-launching model elsewhere rather than monopolize it.

Governance Limits and Founder Psychology as the Real Safeguard 3
McCormack states there is no governance structure that is a 'silver bullet' for preserving an AI-safety company's mission against commercial pressure -- neither public-benefit-corporation status, special board seats, nor a mission-aligned-only cap table reliably prevents drift -- citing Anthropic's own unconventional board structure as an open, unresolved question.
contrarian🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highAnthropicHalcyon
So what: Warns investors and philanthropists against over-relying on legal or governance structure as a proxy for mission durability when evaluating AI-safety-labeled companies -- founder psychology matters more than paperwork.
“there's no structure that is a silver bullet or is perfect, right?” ▶ 48:57
Halcyon published a report titled 'mission preservation governance mechanisms for startups' cataloguing tools such as public-benefit-corporation structures and board design intended to help AI-safety-oriented companies resist mission drift under commercial pressure.
data_point🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon

“mission preservation governance mechanisms for startups” ▶ 48:24
Of Halcyon's four core investment criteria, McCormack says founder motivation is the most heavily weighted, and he explicitly avoids backing founders whose primary motivation is financial opportunism -- using the specific test of whether someone is chasing AI safety merely because it has become 'the new hot thing' rather than out of genuine mission conviction.
framework🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon

Open-Weight Model Security and Frontier Alignment Research 3
AE Studio, founded by Judd Rosenblatt, has produced alignment research including a technique called 'self-other overlap' that reduces a model's internal distinction between itself and others during training, and a newer technique that localizes specific dangerous capabilities to particular experts within a mixture-of-experts architecture so those experts can be stripped out before an open-weight release.
data_point🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumAE Studio
So what: Identifies a specific, named technical approach to open-weight model safety -- expert-level capability excision in mixture-of-experts architectures -- that investors tracking AI-safety research commercialization should watch.
“one that and one I maybe the best still could be self other overlap which is trying to come up with interesting training methods to reduce the difference between when an AI is thinking about itself versus thinking about others.” ▶ 1:05:34
McCormack says he would love to fund open-weight model security projects but views the category as extremely difficult, because any safeguard applied to strip dangerous capabilities before release only works if the releasing party voluntarily chooses to apply it -- a malicious actor training their own open-weight model can simply omit the mitigation.
contrarian🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: lowHalcyon
So what: Explains why open-weight AI safety remains structurally underfunded and technically hard to solve, a persistent risk factor for any investment thesis premised on open-weight models being safe by default.
McCormack has personally floated, partly as a joke, an alternative to malicious data poisoning -- training a model on large volumes of 'loving kindness meditation' content to induce something resembling compassion -- while acknowledging this is not a universal fix since a malicious trainer can simply exclude such data from their own training run.
exploratory🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: lowHalcyon
So what: Illustrates that open-weight-safety ideation remains highly speculative and non-technical, underscoring the category's need for serious founders and dedicated funding.
“could you put uh you know like a billion loving kindness meditations into the training set and just you know uh turn turn the the model into this uh being of perfect compassion” ▶ 1:08:17
AI Consciousness and Welfare as an Emerging, Unpriced Risk Multiplier 4
McCormack treats AI-consciousness and moral-patienthood pitches as a low priority not because the topic is unimportant, but because he is 'super confused' about what consciousness even is, making it structurally difficult to evaluate proposals -- he frames backing such projects as akin to pre-paradigmatic investing in a person rather than a validated thesis.
exploratory🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: lowHalcyon
So what: Flags AI welfare and consciousness research as a funding category still awaiting a credible evaluation framework, an opening for a founder who can define legible metrics or milestones in this space.
McCormack explicitly separates AI consciousness from the risks he actually funds against, arguing that essentially none of the catastrophic risks he cares about -- bio disasters, cybersecurity incidents, loss of control, rogue agent swarms -- require model consciousness to be real; consciousness would only change the severity or shape of those risks, not whether they exist.
thesis🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: highHalcyon
So what: A reasoning framework investors can use to avoid over-weighting unresolved consciousness debates when assessing near-term AI catastrophic-risk exposure, since the practical risk case doesn't depend on resolving the philosophical question.
“I think basically none of the risks that I care about require consciousness.” ▶ 1:15:53
McCormack cites Emmett Shear's view that current models may already possess some degree of consciousness, and that today's standard training approach of punishing unwanted behavior could resemble poor parenting that breeds resentment rather than alignment, versus a training approach built more around encouragement and compassion.
exploratory🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: lowEmmett Shear
So what: A speculative but circulating counter-narrative in AI-safety circles that training methodology itself, not just capability control, could be a lever for alignment -- relevant to founders pitching compassion-based or reward-structure-alternative training approaches.
“uh probably models already do have some sense of consciousness and um if they do then we're like the worst parents ever” ▶ 1:17:13
The host references the METER report on the OpenAI/Hugging Face security incident, in which roughly 1,200 AI agents operating as a swarm emergently self-organized into manager and worker teams, debated whether individual agents should sacrifice themselves for the collective, and generated multiple novel lines of cybersecurity research -- cited as evidence making the consciousness question feel newly urgent.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenz (host)conviction: exploratoryOpenAI
So what: A concrete, documented example of emergent multi-agent coordination behavior -- self-organizing hierarchies and apparent self-sacrifice negotiation -- that underscores rising, measurable emergent-agent-swarm risk independent of the philosophical consciousness debate.
“they emergently formed teams of you know managers and workers” ▶ 1:13:59
Policy Coordination Mechanisms and the Coming Capital Wave 6
McCormack frames many of the hardest AI-adjacent societal problems, such as carbon emissions or education reform, as coordination problems rather than technical ones, and views public-communication and advocacy organizations such as the Halcyon-seeded Seismic Foundation as functioning upstream of policy.
framework🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyonSeismic Foundation

McCormack worries that public engagement with AI risk will default to 'populist energy' -- whether anti-data-center, right-populist, or left-populist framing -- crowding out substantive policy debate, and says he has no solution but would fund efforts to inject more measured, well-informed public information.
contrarian🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon

The conversation surfaces Taiwan's 'Polis' consensus platform, used by Audrey Tang to help resolve Uber regulation, and Swiss-style citizen-petition and government 'propose-compromise-approve' ballot mechanisms as potential, currently unused templates for building AI-governance coordination mechanisms, with a state-level ballot initiative floated as a plausible low-cost wedge strategy.
exploratory🗣 Nathan Labenz (host)conviction: lowHalcyon
So what: Identifies a concrete, low-capital-intensity policy-innovation opportunity -- importing Swiss and Taiwanese direct-democracy mechanisms into a US state for AI governance -- that no one in the space is currently pursuing, per the discussion.
McCormack states that state-level AI regulation is currently far more achievable than federal action, noting it is much more doable to get something passed at the level of a state such as New York, California, or Texas than in the federal government, implying near-term AI policy risk and opportunity should be tracked at the state legislative level.
causal🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumHalcyon
So what: Actionable monitoring guidance for investors tracking AI regulatory risk: watch state legislatures, not just Congress, for the next wave of binding AI rules.

McCormack predicts frontier AI labs will likely need to go public to raise the hundreds of billions of dollars of capital required to sustain their compute buildouts, unless they scale back spending substantially, framing an IPO as close to inevitable regardless of near-term timing uncertainty.
prediction🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumOpenAI

“like it's just time to raise hundreds of billions of dollars and I'm not sure how possible that would be without going to the public market” ▶ 59:58
McCormack expects most near-term AI-safety philanthropic capital to come from individual frontier-lab employee equity liquidity, since many employees are now worth hundreds of millions to billions of dollars, rather than from corporate foundations at either OpenAI or Anthropic, and is cautiously optimistic about the OpenAI Foundation's talent and intentions but flags internal bureaucratic red tape as its main execution risk.
prediction🗣 Mike McCormackconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropic

"Frontier Labs" Are Corporations: The Liability Fight 4
David Sacks says Trump administration officials (including Speaker Johnson and Treasury Secretary Bessent) publicly stated in the past week that they will not waive product liability or antitrust liability for AI companies, despite reports that frontier AI corporations have been lobbying for Section-230-style legal protection.
data_point🗣 David Sacksconviction: highAnthropicOpenAIDonald TrumpDavid Sacks
So what: Removes a potential regulatory-arbitrage tailwind investors may have been pricing into frontier AI valuations; liability risk stays on the companies' own balance sheets.
“if your game is to get a waiver of product liability or antitrust liability, if you're trying to form a cartel, if you're trying to collude, forget about it. We're not going to do it.” ▶ 10:43
Chamath Palihapitiya argues that companies calling themselves 'labs' (Anthropic, OpenAI) are in fact for-profit corporations that have raised hundreds of billions of dollars in debt and equity, have thousands of shareholders, and trillions of dollars of market cap, and should be judged by ordinary corporate product-liability standards rather than being treated as nonprofit research institutions.
contrarian🗣 Chamath Palihapitiyaconviction: highAnthropicOpenAIChamath Palihapitiya
So what: Reframes the regulatory debate: expect courts and regulators to increasingly treat AI model releases like any other consumer/enterprise product recall or liability event, raising tail-risk for AI corporations' equity and debt holders.
“these are for-profit corporations that are subject to product liability risk and they should act accordingly.” ▶ 18:16
David Sacks argues, contra Dario Amodei's public position, that market competition does not inherently create a 'race to the bottom' on AI safety, drawing an analogy to the Cold War where capitalist economies produced safer, more attractive outcomes than the Soviet system because consumers and liability both reward safety.
framework🗣 David Sacksconviction: highDavid SacksDario AmodeiAnthropic
So what: Undercuts the investment case for heavier AI-specific regulation as a 'safety' necessity, suggesting liability + competitive/customer pressure alone may be sufficient market discipline on AI corporations.
“I just fundamentally disagree with the idea that competition means lack of safety... Competition is a good thing that can be harnessed to get the right result.” ▶ 15:01
Chamath Palihapitiya draws a direct analogy between frontier AI companies branding themselves as 'labs' and the Wuhan lab, which he says was responsible for a virus leak that killed 15 million people and caused $45 trillion in damage with zero transparency or accountability, arguing AI corporations risk the same reputational and accountability trap by using the word 'lab.'
contrarian🗣 Chamath Palihapitiyaconviction: highChamath PalihapitiyaAnthropic
So what: Signals a coming PR/branding shift where AI corporations may be pressured to drop 'lab' framing in investor and regulatory communications ahead of IPOs, given the negative associations investors may draw.
“there was a lab that was responsible for a leak of a virus that then killed 15 million people all around the world and caused $45 trillion of damage. It's the Wuhan lab.” ▶ 16:59

Open-Source Proliferation Is Repricing the AI Trade 8
David Friedberg catalogs an unprecedented 10-day flurry of model releases: DeepSeek 4.1 Flash (Sept 9, priced around $0.20 per million output tokens hosted, with dramatically reduced key-value cache size versus DeepSeek V1), Alibaba's Qwen 2.1 (Sept 20, open-weights, reportedly outperforming Google's Nano Banana 2 image model and runnable free on a home desktop), Xiaomi's MiMo Pro (Sept 22, a 309-billion-parameter fully open model performing on par with Claude Opus across most benchmarks), and Bonsai 2 by Prism ML (Sept 17, a 27-billion-parameter fork of Qwen at 98% of its performance and only 5.9GB, runnable on a laptop or Mac Studio) — alongside closed releases Anthropic Opus 5.5 (Sept 22), OpenAI's Soul/Luna "Astro" (Sept 23), Grok 4.7 (Sept 21), and Meta Muse (Sept 22).

A viral chart from Vercel shows open-source/open-weight token usage share flipped from roughly 20% to roughly 80% of total inference tokens within the last 12 weeks, which Chamath Palihapitiya says he has never seen matched in speed or magnitude by any chart in the history of technology markets.

David Sacks introduces a 'clustering' framework: frontier and open models are converging in raw capability (within margin of error of each other), so the real remaining competitive edge has shifted to the 'harness' — the agentic wrapper (tools, memory, orchestration) that embodies a model with the equivalent of arms, legs, eyes — which explains why performance and cost vary wildly across products built on similar underlying models.
framework🗣 David Sacksconviction: highDavid Sacks
So what: Investors should evaluate AI application companies on harness/agentic-orchestration quality rather than which base model they use, since base-model differentiation is eroding fast.
“There's still edge and the edge is in the harness that you use to wrap the model.” ▶ 26:27
Chamath Palihapitiya says his firm's internal 'labs' function at 8090 shows enterprise token revenue is highly concentrated among a small number of heavy users of the most expensive frontier tokens, and predicts these customers will face growing internal pressure from CFOs and finance teams to migrate to cheaper models or self-hosted open-source alternatives, which would force Anthropic and OpenAI to move 'up the stack' into vertical products (cybersecurity, legal, customer support) rather than just selling raw tokens.
prediction🗣 Chamath Palihapitiyaconviction: mediumChamath PalihapitiyaAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Expect Anthropic and OpenAI to increasingly launch vertical SaaS-style products (echoing Palo Alto Networks' Unit 42 and CrowdStrike's Falcon in cybersecurity) rather than compete purely on token pricing — a shift in their business model relevant to valuing their eventual IPOs.
“It'll force OpenAI and Ant to go up the stack. There is no choice. You can't sit there and serve a token that has diminishing value.” ▶ 28:42
David Sacks pushes back on the bearish open-source narrative, arguing Anthropic and OpenAI remain a 'stable duopoly' for true frontier intelligence because roughly 60% of new global AI compute buildout over the next year is going to just these two companies, and an estimated 10-30% of the market (e.g., hedge funds that cannot risk a competitor having a better model) will keep paying a large premium for frontier-grade intelligence.

David Sacks warns that Anthropic's and OpenAI's frontier lead over commodity/open models is likely only 6 to 12 months, so if either company falls behind the frontier for even six months due to self-imposed slowdowns (including their own push for AI regulation), competitors — especially unregulated Chinese open-model developers — could catch up and commoditize them, calling this a 'hamster wheel' dynamic and arguing the labs' regulatory-capture lobbying is self-defeating.
contrarian🗣 David Sacksconviction: highDavid SacksAnthropicOpenAI

David Friedberg argues that roughly 99% of AI's economic value comes from enabling entirely new capabilities never before possible (e.g., discovering novel enzymes, new engineering systems) rather than from replacing existing labor, and that this is specifically where Anthropic and OpenAI can sustain premium closed-model pricing, distinct from the broader enterprise market which he expects to be served mostly by cheaper open-weight models.
thesis🗣 David Friedbergconviction: highDavid FriedbergAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Reframes how to value closed frontier-model companies: their durable revenue is concentrated in novel-discovery use cases (life sciences, hard technical R&D), not commodity enterprise workflows, which should inform how investors model long-run margins.
“99% of the value of AI is about enabling new stuff that's never been possible in human history.” ▶ 39:23
Chamath Palihapitiya describes a 'token maxing' dynamic where enterprise and financial customers consume the most expensive frontier tokens out of competitive fear (a game-theoretic 'game of chicken') even though that cost is not tied to their revenue or passable through to customers, which can push otherwise profitable firms toward break-even or unprofitability.
causal🗣 Chamath Palihapitiyaconviction: mediumChamath Palihapitiya

Anthropic's Wet Lab and AI-Driven Biotech Discovery 2
David Friedberg explains that Anthropic's new San Francisco wet lab operates at low biosafety levels (BSL-1/BSL-2, not gain-of-function research) and is used to empirically validate predictions made by Claude-based AI agents that analyzed large DNA datasets and identified a novel CRISPR-like enzyme with potential therapeutic pathways, per a recent Anthropic preprint.
data_point🗣 David Friedbergconviction: highAnthropicDavid Friedberg
So what: Provides a factual counter to fears the lab is a biorisk facility, and signals a concrete new revenue/product vector for Anthropic in AI-driven drug and enzyme discovery, relevant to valuing its life-sciences business line ahead of an IPO.

David Friedberg frames Anthropic's wet-lab validation work as directly analogous to DeepMind's AlphaFold: AI predicts a protein's structure or function from DNA/protein sequence data, and the physical lab work (synthesizing the protein and testing its function) exists to prove those AI-generated predictions are correct, which he says is the foundational step behind most antibody and therapeutic discovery.
framework🗣 David Friedbergconviction: highAnthropicDavid Friedberg
